We warn the reader that, in particular, this relation means that the operators
xD:\Fil_{\bullet}V\rightarrow\Fil_{\bullet}V
are
not
compatible with the filtration; however, since we are working over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and hence
xD-p=xD
, the operator
xD

is
compatible with the coarser filtration

\Fil_{p\bullet}V=(\dots\rightarrow\Fil_{-p}V\rightarrow\Fil_{0}V\rightarrow\Fil_{p}V\rightarrow\dots)\;.

Under the above description of
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}})
, the Breuil–Kisin twist
\mathcal{O}\{n\}
corresponds to the filtered complex
\Fil_{\bullet}V
with
\Fil_{\bullet}V=\mathbb{F}_{p}
if
\bullet\geq-n
and zero else with the transition maps between nonzero terms being the identity; moreover, the operator
D:\Fil_{i}V\rightarrow\Fil_{i-1}V
is given by multiplication by
i
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

If
E
corresponds to a graded
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module
M
, then restriction to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}
corresponds to passing to
(M[1/x^{p}]_{\deg=0},\Theta=xD)
and one may view this as passing from
\Fil_{p\bullet}V
to the underlying non-filtered complex and taking
\Theta=xD
. Restriction to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}
, however, corresponds to passing to the associated graded
\gr_{\bullet}V
of
\Fil_{\bullet}V
and taking
\Theta=D^{p}
. Finally, we have

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}},E)=\fib(\Fil_{0}V\xrightarrow{D}\Fil_{-1}V)\;.

(ii)

For
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}\cong B_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}\mathcal{G}
, one can show that any
E\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}})
corresponds to a
decreasingly
filtered complex
\Fil^{\bullet}V\in\mathcal{DF}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
together with an operator
\Theta:\Fil^{\bullet}V\rightarrow\Fil^{\bullet-p}V
such that the action of
\Theta^{p}-t^{p^{2}-p}\Theta
on
\Rees(\Fil^{\bullet}V)
is locally nilpotent in cohomology, see
[
6
, Prop. 6.5.6]
. Under this correspondence, the Breuil–Kisin twist
\mathcal{O}\{n\}
corresponds to the filtered complex
\Fil^{\bullet}V
with
\Fil^{\bullet}V=\mathbb{F}_{p}
if
\bullet\leq-n
and zero else with the transition maps between nonzero terms being the identity and
\Theta=n
. For any
E\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}})
corresponding to
(\Fil^{\bullet}V,\Theta)
, restriction to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}
corresponds to passage to the underlying non-filtered complex and restriction to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}
corresponds to passage to the associated graded; moreover, we have

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}},E)=\fib(\Fil^{0}V\xrightarrow{\Theta}\Fil^{-p}V)\;.

For
E\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, let us introduce the notation
F_{\mathrm{dR}}(E)
for
R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}},E|_{\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}})
and define
F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(E),F_{\mathrm{HT},c}(E)
and
F_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)
analogously. Then we have natural restriction maps

\begin{split}a_{E}:F_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)&\rightarrow F_{\mathrm{dR}}(E)\oplus F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(E)\;,\\
b_{E}:F_{\mathrm{HT},c}(E)&\rightarrow F_{\mathrm{dR}}(E)\oplus F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(E)\end{split}

and
the gluing description of
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
shows that

(2.4.6)

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)=\fib(F_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)\oplus F_{\mathrm{HT},c}(E)\xrightarrow{a_{E}-b_{E}}F_{\mathrm{dR}}(E)\oplus F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(E))\;.

As we now have a good handle on cohomology on
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, we want to make use of this to understand cohomology on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
.

Definition 2.4.19
.

Let
E\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
. The filtration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E\{-(p-1)\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E\{p-1\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}

is called the
syntomic filtration
and denoted
\Fil_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{Syn}}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\frac{1}{v_{1}}]
; here,
E\{n\}
denotes the tensor product
E\otimes\mathcal{O}\{n\}
for any
n
. We denote the underlying unfiltered object by
R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\frac{1}{v_{1}}]
.

As one can show that
v_{1}
is topologically nilpotent, the syntomic filtration is complete, see
Proposition

4.1.3
. Moreover, notice
that, for any
E\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
, there is a canonical isomorphism

(2.4.7)

\gr^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\bullet}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\cong R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E/v_{1}\{\bullet(p-1)\})\;,

where
E/v_{1}\coloneqq\cofib(E\{-(p-1)\}\xrightarrow{v_{1}}E)
. Moreover,
if
E\in\Perf((\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
, one can check that there is a natural isomorphism

(2.4.8)

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\cong R\Gamma(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))\;,

see
[
6
, Eq. (6.4.1)]
.

3.
The stacky Beilinson fibre square

Recall the following theorem of Antieau–Mathew–Morrow–Nikolaus relating syntomic cohomology to Hodge-filtered de Rham cohomology:

Theorem 3.0.1
.

Let
X
be a
smooth qcqs

p
-adic formal scheme. For each
i\geq 0
, there is a functorial pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p}(i))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X_{p=0},\mathbb{Z}_{p}(i))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fil^{i}_{\mathrm{Hod}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)[\frac{1}{p}]\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Proof.

The affine version is
[
3
, Thm. 6.17]
and we deduce the general version using Zariski descent and
Lemma

A.0.3
.

∎

We want to prove a stacky version of this result and thereby also generalise it to allow arbitrary coefficients, at least in the case where
X
is proper.
Namely, the main theorem we are going to prove is the following:

Theorem 3.0.2
.

There is a commutative square of stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{dR},+}}

which is an almost pushout up to
p
-isogeny.
More precisely,
for any
E\in\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, it induces a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

This allows us to generalise
Theorem

3.0.1
to accommodate
F
-gauge coefficients in the case where
X
is proper in the following way:

Corollary 3.0.3
.

Let
X
be a
p
-adic formal scheme which is smooth and proper over
\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. For any perfect
F
-gauge
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is a natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,E)[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X_{p=0},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Fil^{0}_{\mathrm{Hod}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Proof.

Using
Proposition

B.0.1
, this follows immediately from
Theorem

3.0.2
.
∎

In particular, by the comparison from
Theorem

2.2.10
, the above recovers the result of
Theorem

3.0.1

in the proper case
by putting
E=\mathcal{O}\{i\}
.

3.1.
Construction of the square

We start by constructing the stacky Beilinson fibre square. Namely, using the open immersion
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+}
from
Remark

2.2.8
and the isomorphism
\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\cong\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}
from
Example

2.3.5
, we obtain a square

(3.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{dR},+}}

Proposition 3.1.1
.

The diagram (
3.1.1
) is commutative.

Proof.

Clearly, it suffices to show that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{dR},+}}

commutes and this equates to showing that the composite map
\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\xrightarrow{j_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
identifies with the de Rham map
i_{\mathrm{dR}}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
. By naturality of the map
j_{\mathrm{dR}}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathrm{dR}}}

commutes and thus we may consider the composition
\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}\xrightarrow{j_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
instead. However, under the identification
\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\cong\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
from
Example

2.3.5
, the map
\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}
identifies with the map
\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}
sending any
p
-nilpotent ring
S
to the Cartier–Witt divisor
W(S)\xrightarrow{p}W(S)
by the proof of
[
6
, Prop. 5.4.2]
and this shows that the composition
\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}\xrightarrow{j_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
identifies with
i_{\mathrm{dR}}:\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
, as claimed.
∎

3.2.
A Beilinson-type fibre square for crystalline representations

Our strategy to prove
Theorem

3.0.2
will be to make use of
Proposition

2.4.15
. Thus, we will start by quickly reviewing some of the theory of crystalline representations. For more details on this topic, we refer to
[
22
]
.

Recall that we use
C
to denote a
fixed
completed algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and recall the rings

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{\mathrm{inf}}\coloneqq A_{\mathrm{inf}}(\mathcal{O}_{C})=W(\mathcal{O}_{C}^{\flat})\;,\hskip 14.22636ptA_{\mathrm{crys}}\coloneqq A_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathcal{O}_{C})=A_{\mathrm{inf}}[\tfrac{\xi^{n}}{n!}:n\geq 0]_{(p)}^{\wedge}}

from
Example

2.2.2
, where
\xi

is a generator of
the kernel of the Fontaine map
\theta:A_{\mathrm{inf}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}
. Choosing once and for all a compatible system
\varepsilon=(1,\zeta_{p},\zeta_{p^{2}},\dots)\in\mathcal{O}_{C}^{\flat}
of
p
-power roots of unity, one can actually take

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi=\frac{[\varepsilon]-1}{[\varepsilon^{1/p}]-1}=1+[\varepsilon^{1/p}]+\dots+[\varepsilon^{(p-1)/p}]\;,}

where
[\hskip 2.0pt\cdot\hskip 2.0pt]:\mathcal{O}_{C}^{\flat}\rightarrow W(\mathcal{O}_{C}^{\flat})
is the Teichmüller representative.
One can show that

A_{\mathrm{crys}}
contains
the element

(3.2.1)

t=\log[\varepsilon]=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1-[\varepsilon])^{k}}{k}\;,

see
[
22
, Prop. 7.6]
,
and
thus
we define rings

B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+}\coloneqq A_{\mathrm{crys}}[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;,\hskip 14.22636ptB_{\mathrm{crys}}\coloneqq B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+}[\tfrac{1}{t}]\;;

note that one can actually show that
B_{\mathrm{crys}}=A_{\mathrm{crys}}[\frac{1}{t}]
. The ring
B_{\mathrm{crys}}
is equipped with a natural
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-action and, perhaps most importantly, the Frobenius
\varphi
of
A_{\mathrm{inf}}
can be continued to an endomorphism of
B_{\mathrm{crys}}
. We moreover define a ring

B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\coloneqq A_{\mathrm{inf}}[\tfrac{1}{p}]_{(\xi)}^{\wedge}\;,

which is a complete discrete valuation ring with uniformiser
\xi
and hence its fraction field
B_{\mathrm{dR}}\coloneqq B_{\mathrm{dR}}[\tfrac{1}{\xi}]
is equipped with a natural descending
\xi
-adic
filtration

\Fil^{i}B_{\mathrm{dR}}\coloneqq\xi^{i}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\;.

Note that
B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+}\subseteq B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
and
B_{\mathrm{crys}}\subseteq B_{\mathrm{dR}}
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

A finite-dimensional
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representation
V
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is called
crystalline
if there is a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-equivariant isomorphism

V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}\cong B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{\dim V}\;.

We use
\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{crys}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
to denote the category of crystalline
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representations.

For a crystalline representation
V
, we write

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)\coloneqq(V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\;,

where
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
acts diagonally.
From

B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}=B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
see
[
22
, Prop. 6.26]
, we conclude that

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space of dimension
\dim V
. By virtue of the Frobenius on
B_{\mathrm{crys}}
, it comes equipped with a natural Frobenius action
\varphi
and due to

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)=(V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\;,

which again comes from
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}=B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
and
V
being crystalline
, it also inherits a natural filtration from
B_{\mathrm{dR}}
. Thus,
D_{\mathrm{crys}}
defines a functor

D_{\mathrm{crys}}:\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{crys}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\rightarrow\MF^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

from crystalline representations into the category of filtered
\varphi
-modules over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, which is defined as follows:

Definition 3.2.2
.

A
\varphi
-module
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space
M
together with the data of an isomorphism
\varphi^{*}M\cong M
, where
\varphi
denotes the Frobenius on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and we denote the category of
\varphi
-modules over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
by
\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. A
filtered
\varphi
-module
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is a filtered
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space
D\in\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\coloneqq\Fun((\mathbb{Z},\geq),\Mod(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
equipped with the data of a
\varphi
-module structure on
\underline{D}=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{i}D(i)
.

Note that our definition of a filtered
\varphi
-module differs slightly from the usual one in that we also allow non-honest filtrations (i.e. filtrations with non-injective transition maps) and that we furthermore do not require the filtration to be
complete
as one would usually do. This has the advantage that the category
\MF^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is clearly abelian (while this is not the case with the usual definition) and it is also more convenient for our goal of proving
Theorem

3.0.2
.

It is a theorem of Fontaine that the functor
D_{\mathrm{crys}}
defines an equivalence from
\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{crys}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
onto an abelian subcategory of
\MF^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denoted
\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
,
see
[
23
, §5.5.2.(iii)]
,
and a filtered
\varphi
-module in the essential image of
D_{\mathrm{crys}}
is called
admissible
.

Note that we have exact forgetful functors

\begin{split}T_{\mathrm{dR},+}:\MF^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})&\rightarrow\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\;,\\
T_{\mathrm{crys}}:\MF^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})&\rightarrow\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\;,\\
T_{\mathrm{dR}}:\MF^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})&\rightarrow\Mod(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\end{split}

defined by forgetting the
\varphi
-structure, the filtration or both, respectively. Note that we use the same notation as for the realisation functors for
F
-gauges, but it will always be clear from the context which functor we mean. Our main goal in this section is to prove the following statement:

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{D}^{b}(\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
be a bounded complex of admissible filtered
\varphi
-modules. Then the forgetful functors above induce a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(D))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Note that, as the category
\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
does not have enough injectives
(in fact, it does not have any nonzero injective object by
[
4
, Rem. 1.9]
)
, the notation
\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)
does not make sense a priori. However, whenever we have an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
, we can embed it into its Ind-category
\Ind(\mathcal{A})
and this will have enough injectives. By
[
36
, Thm. 3.5]
, we have

\Ext^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(M,N)\cong H^{i}(\RHom_{\Ind(\mathcal{A})}(M,N))\;,

where we use Yoneda Ext on the left-hand side, and hence we may define

\RHom_{\mathcal{A}}(M,N)\coloneqq\RHom_{\Ind(\mathcal{A})}(M,N)

as this recovers the usual definition in the case where
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives.

Proof of
Proposition

3.2.3
.

First observe that we can readily calculate

\begin{split}\RHom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(D))&=\underline{D}\;,\\
\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(D))&=\Fil^{0}\underline{D}\;,\\
\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(D))&=\underline{D}^{\varphi=1}\;,\end{split}

where

\underline{D}^{\varphi=1}\coloneqq\fib(\underline{D}\xrightarrow{\varphi-1}\underline{D})

denotes the derived
\varphi
-invariants of
\underline{D}
,
and hence we only need to show that

\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)\cong\fib(\underline{D}^{\varphi=1}\rightarrow\underline{D}/\Fil^{0}\underline{D})\;,

where the quotient
\underline{D}/\Fil^{0}\underline{D}
is to be taken in the derived sense, i.e.

\underline{D}/\Fil^{0}\underline{D}\coloneqq\cofib(\Fil^{0}\underline{D}\rightarrow\underline{D})\;.

However, this follows from
[
19
, Cor. 2.4.4]
.
∎

Remark 3.2.4
.

The above proof also shows that, if
D
is an admissible filtered
\varphi
-module, then the complex
\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)
is concentrated in degrees
0
and
1
:
Indeed, in this case the map
\Fil^{0}\underline{D}\rightarrow\underline{D}
is injective and hence
\underline{D}/\Fil^{0}\underline{D}
is concentrated in degree zero.

3.3.
Étale realisation and the Beilinson fibre square

Recall from
Proposition

2.4.15
that, for any
E\in\Coh(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, the Galois representation
T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]
is crystalline. Hence, we can associate to
E
a filtered
\varphi
-module
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])
and, in this section, we will show how this filtered
\varphi
-module can also be obtained in a geometric manner.

To explain this, recall from
Example

2.4.4
that an
F
-gauge
E
on
\mathbb{F}_{p}
corresponds to a diagram

(3.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

of
p
-complete
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-complexes such that
ut=tu=p
together with an isomorphism
\tau:\varphi^{*}M^{\infty}\cong M^{-\infty}
. Base changing everything to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we see that all maps in the diagram above become isomorphisms and the only remaining data is
M^{-\infty}
together with the isomorphism
\varphi^{*}M^{-\infty}[\frac{1}{p}]\cong M^{-\infty}[\frac{1}{p}]
induced by
\tau
. Sending
E
to
M^{-\infty}[\frac{1}{p}]
equipped with its Frobenius structure determines a functor

(3.3.2)

(-)[\tfrac{1}{p}]:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})\rightarrow\Mod_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}[x,x^{-1}]}(\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))\cong\mathcal{D}(\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))\;,

where the last equivalence is due to
[
32
, Thm. 7.1.3.1]
, and we remind the reader that
the restriction of this functor to
\Perf(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
exactly recovers
the functor from (
2.4.3
).
Finally, recalling that the pullback
j{\Delta}^{*}E\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta})\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, where we have used
Example

2.3.5
, identifies with
M^{-\infty}
, we see that the functor (
3.3.2
) yields a commutative diagram

(3.3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta})\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))}
,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j{\Delta}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)[\tfrac{1}{p}]}

where
\mathcal{D}(\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is the forgetful functor.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

For any
E\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, the functor
(-)[\frac{1}{p}]
induces an isomorphism

R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},E[\tfrac{1}{p}])\;.

Proof.

Recall from
Example

2.4.4
that

R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)=\fib(M^{0}\xrightarrow{t^{\infty}-\tau u^{\infty}}M^{-\infty})

in the above notation. However, as
t^{\infty}
and
u^{\infty}
become isomorphisms upon inverting
p
, this yields

R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\fib(M^{-\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{1-\varphi}M^{-\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}])\cong\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M^{-\infty}[\tfrac{1}{p}])\;,

so we are done.
∎

Lemma 3.3.2
.

For any
E\in\Coh(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is a natural isomorphism

T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong T_{\mathrm{crys}}(D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]))\;.

Proof.

Since any coherent
F
-gauge on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is isomorphic to a reflexive
F
-gauge up to
p
-isogeny by
[
6
, Prop. 6.7.1]
, we may assume that
E
is reflexive. Let
\mathcal{E}\in\Vect^{\varphi}((\mathbb{Z}_{p}){\Delta})
denote the prismatic
F
-crystal in vector bundles corresponding to
E
by
Theorem

2.4.14
.
Identifying
T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E)
with a diagram (
3.3.1
) and writing
M^{-\infty}\coloneqq(\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{i}M^{-i})_{(p)}^{\wedge}
as usual, the module

M^{-\infty}
identifies with the pullback of
\mathcal{E}
to the absolute prismatic site of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, i.e.
T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})[\frac{1}{p}]
, so we have to show

(3.3.4)

\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}

as
\varphi
-modules. Recalling that

T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)=(\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}W(C^{\flat}))^{\varphi=1}

from (
2.4.4
),
we see that
[
11
, Lem. 4.26]
implies that there is a natural isomorphism

(3.3.5)

T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}A_{\mathrm{inf}}[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\tfrac{1}{\mu}]\;,

where
\mu\coloneqq[\varepsilon^{1/p}]-1
; note that our definition of
\mu
differs from the one in loc. cit. by a Frobenius twist since not
\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})
, but
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})
is a Breuil–Kisin–Fargues module in the sense of
[
11
, Def. 4.22]
.
Using the maps
\mathcal{O}_{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}/p\leftarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}
and the fact that
(A_{\mathrm{crys}},(p))
is the initial object of the absolute prismatic site of
\mathcal{O}_{C}/p
, see
[
14
, Not. 5.1]
, we obtain natural maps of prisms

(A_{\mathrm{inf}},(\xi))\rightarrow(A_{\mathrm{crys}},(p))\leftarrow(\mathbb{Z}_{p},(p))

and hence
\mathcal{E}
being a prismatic crystal yields isomorphisms

(3.3.6)

\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}A_{\mathrm{crys}}\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{crys}})\cong\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}A_{\mathrm{crys}}\;.

Finally, as

\mu
is invertible in
B_{\mathrm{crys}}
(note that
t\in\xi\mu B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+}
), we overall obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}\cong\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}\;.}

As this isomorphism is both
\varphi
- and
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-equivariant, taking
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-fixed points yields (
3.3.4
).
∎

Observe that there is also a functor

(3.3.7)

(-)[\tfrac{1}{p}]:\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+})\cong\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\widehat{\mathcal{DF}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow\mathcal{DF}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

given by sending a
p
-complete filtered complex
M^{\bullet}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
to the filtered complex
M^{\bullet}[\tfrac{1}{p}]
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and that this yields an analogous result to
Lemma

3.3.1
. Namely, we have the following:

Lemma 3.3.3
.

For any
E\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+})
, the functor
(-)[\tfrac{1}{p}]
induces an isomorphism

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},E[\tfrac{1}{p}])\;.}

Proof.

Identifying
E
with a
p
-complete filtered complex
M^{\bullet}
via the Rees equivalence, this follows by observing that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong M^{0}[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},E[\tfrac{1}{p}])\;.}\qed

Furthermore, we also get a statement analogous to
Lemma

3.3.2
:

Lemma 3.3.4
.

For any
E\in\Coh(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is a natural isomorphism

T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]))\;.

Proof.

As in the proof of of
Lemma

3.3.2
, we may assume that
E
is reflexive and let
\mathcal{E}\in\Vect^{\varphi}((\mathbb{Z}_{p}){\Delta})
denote the corresponding prismatic
F
-crystal in vector bundles. Recall that the filtration on

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\tfrac{1}{p}]})=(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}=(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}

is given by

\Fil^{i}D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))=(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\xi^{i}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\;.

Furthermore, note that the filtered object
T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)
can be defined by descent from
T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E_{\mathcal{O}_{C}})
, where
E_{\mathcal{O}_{C}}
denotes the pullback of
E
to
\mathcal{O}_{C}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. Now observe that, identifying
E_{\mathcal{O}_{C}}
with a graded
A_{\mathrm{inf}}[u,t]/(ut-\varphi^{-1}(\xi))
-module
M
using
Example

2.4.4
, the calculation from
Example

2.2.9
implies that
T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E_{\mathcal{O}_{C}})
corresponds to the graded
A_{\mathrm{inf}}[\frac{u^{n}}{n!},t:n\geq 1]/(ut-\xi)
-module
\varphi^{*}M\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}A_{\mathrm{crys}}
. Hence, by
Proposition

2.4.12
and
Remark

2.4.13
, we conclude that

T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]=(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\;,

where
the filtration on
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+}
is the tensor product filtration and, in turn, the filtration on
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})
is given by

\Fil^{i}\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})=\xi^{i}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\cap\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\;;

here,
the intersection takes place inside
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\frac{1}{\xi}]\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\frac{1}{\xi}]
via the Frobenius structure on
\mathcal{E}
. Using (
3.3.6
), however, we see that

\begin{split}(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}&\cong(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}}^{+})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\cong\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\\
&\cong(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\cong(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\end{split}

and hence we also have

T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]=(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\;.

We conclude that it suffices to show that the isomorphism

(3.3.8)

T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}

obtained from (
3.3.5
) using
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\frac{1}{\xi}]\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\frac{1}{\xi}]
is compatible with the filtrations,
where again the right-hand side is equipped with the tensor product filtration.
As both sides are
\xi
-adically filtered,
i.e. their filtrations have the property that
\Fil^{i}=\xi^{i}\Fil^{0}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, it suffices to check this in filtration degree
0
.
In other words,
we have to show that the isomorphism above restricts to an isomorphism

T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\cong\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(\xi^{i}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\cap\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}}))\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}\xi^{-i}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}=\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\;,

where the right-hand side is a submodule of
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}
via the isomorphism
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\frac{1}{\xi}]\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})[\frac{1}{\xi}]
. However, this again follows from (
3.3.5
): As
\mu=[\varepsilon^{1/p}]-1
maps to the nonzero element
\zeta_{p}-1
in the residue field of
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
, it is already invertible in
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
and hence the isomorphism (
3.3.8
) restricts to an isomorphism

T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\cong\mathcal{E}(A_{\mathrm{inf}})\otimes_{A_{\mathrm{inf}}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\;,

as desired.
∎

3.4.
Proof of the main theorem

We are now ready to put everything together and prove
Theorem

3.0.2
. We begin with a general lemma about
t
-structures on stable
\infty
-categories, which is probably well-known, see also
[
13
, Lem. 3.2]
.
For this, first recall the following definition:

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category equipped with a
t
-structure
(\mathcal{C}^{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}^{\leq 0})
and put

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}^{-}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\leq n}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{C}^{+}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\geq-n}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{C}^{b}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}^{-}\cap\mathcal{C}^{+}\;.}

Then the
t
-structure
(\mathcal{C}^{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}^{\leq 0})
is called
bounded
if
\mathcal{C}^{b}=\mathcal{C}
. It is called
non-degenerate
if

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\leq n}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\geq n}=0\;.}

Remark 3.4.2
.

Recall that a
t
-structure
(\mathcal{C}^{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}^{\leq 0})
on a stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
induces canoncial truncation functors

\tau^{\leq n}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\leq n}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt\tau^{\geq n}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\geq n}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
which are right-adjoint, respectively left-adjoint, to the inclusions
\mathcal{C}^{\leq n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, respectively
\mathcal{C}^{\geq n}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, we obtain cohomology functors

H^{n}(-)=(\tau^{\geq n}\circ\tau^{\leq n})(-)[n]:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\geq 0}\cap\mathcal{C}^{\leq 0}=\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}\;.

If the
t
-structure is non-degenerate, this implies that an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
with
H^{n}(C)=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
must vanish. Moreover, it also implies that
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\geq 0}
if and only if
H^{n}(C)=0
for all
n<0
and, similarly,
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\leq 0}
if and only if
H^{n}(C)=0
for all
n>0
, see
[
21
, Rem. 1.5]
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category equipped with a bounded non-degenerate
t
-structure. Then
\mathcal{C}
is generated under shifts and
fibres
by its heart
\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
.

Proof.

By boundedness of the
t
-structure,
any
E\in\mathcal{C}
has only finitely many non-vanishing cohomology groups and we use induction on the number of such nonzero
H^{i}(E)
. If all
H^{i}(E)
vanish, then
E
is itself zero as the
t
-structure is non-degenerate. Otherwise, let
k
be maximal such that
H^{k}(E)\neq 0
and put

E^{\prime}\coloneqq\fib(E\rightarrow\tau^{\leq k-1}E)\;.

As
H^{i}(\tau^{\leq k-1}E)=H^{i}(E)
except for
i=k
, in which case
H^{k}(\tau^{\leq k-1}E)=0
, the long exact cohomology sequence shows that
H^{i}(E^{\prime})=0
for all
i\neq k
and hence
E^{\prime}[k]
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
by non-degeneracy of the
t
-structure. Thus, we are done by induction.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.0.2
.

The relevant square was already constructed in
Proposition

3.1.1
, so it remains to show that it is an almost pushout up to
p
-isogeny.
First note that the
t
-structure on
\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
from
Remark

2.3.19
satisfies the hypotheses of
Lemma

3.4.3
: Indeed, this follows from the fact that this
t
-structure is induced from the one on
\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\langle u,t\rangle)
, which is clearly bounded and non-degenerate. As all terms of the square we claim to be a pullback commute with shifts and fibres, we may thus assume that
E
is coherent by
Lemma

3.4.3
.
In this case,
recalling from
Proposition

2.4.15
that
T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)
is crystalline and
writing
D=D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\frac{1}{p}])
, we know by
Proposition

3.2.3
that there is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(D))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

As

Lemma

3.3.1
,
Lemma

3.3.2
,
Lemma

3.3.3
and
Lemma

3.3.4
allow us to rewrite this as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;,}

it remains to show that
T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}
induces a quasi-isomorphism

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)\;.

For this, first note that the embedding
\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{crys}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\hookrightarrow\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
induces a morphism

\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)\rightarrow R\Gamma(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])

and let
Q
denote its cofibre. We have to show that the composite

(3.4.1)

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow R\Gamma(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])\rightarrow Q\;,

where the first map is induced by
T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}
, is zero. From the long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the last zero is due to the fact that
\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)
is concentrated in degrees
0
and
1

by
Remark

3.2.4
,
we see that
H^{0}(Q)=0
and that
H^{1}(Q)
is the cokernel of the injection
\Ext^{1}_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)\hookrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])
. However, by
Proposition

2.4.15
, the complex
R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]
is concentrated in degrees
0
and
1
and the map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))

factors through
\Ext^{1}_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)
, hence the map in (
3.4.1
) induces the zero map on all cohomology groups and thus is itself zero. This means that we obtain a unique factorisation

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D)\rightarrow R\Gamma(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}])

and, again by
Proposition

2.4.15
, the first map is a quasi-isomorphism, as desired.
∎

3.5.
Comparison with Fontaine–Messing syntomic cohomology

As a corollary of
Theorem

3.0.2
, we can prove a comparison between syntomic cohomology in our sense and the syntomic cohomology introduced by Fontaine–Messing in
[
20
]
.

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
X
be a
smooth qcqs

p
-adic formal scheme. For
0\leq i\leq p-2
, we define the integral
Fontaine–Messing syntomic cohomology
of
X
in weight
i
as

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn},\mathrm{FM}}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p}(i))\coloneqq\fib(\Fil^{i}_{\mathrm{Hod}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)\xrightarrow{1-\varphi/p^{i}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X))

and there is also a rational version defined for all
i\geq 0
by

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn},\mathrm{FM}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\coloneqq\fib(\Fil^{i}_{\mathrm{Hod}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{\varphi-p^{i}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)[\tfrac{1}{p}])\;.

Here,
\varphi
denotes the crystalline Frobenius on
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)
.

Note that the above definition indeed makes sense: By quasisyntomic descent and
[
3
, Prop. 6.8]
, the Frobenius on
\Fil^{i}_{\mathrm{Hod}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)
is divisible by
p^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq p-1
. In loc. cit., Antieau–Mathew–Morrow–Nikolaus prove the following comparison between syntomic cohomology and Fontaine–Messing syntomic cohomology:

Theorem 3.5.2
.

Let
X
be a
smooth qcqs

p
-adic formal scheme. For
i\geq 0
, there is a natural isomorphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn},\mathrm{FM}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\cong R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\;.

Proof.

The affine version is
[
3
, Thm. 6.22]

and we deduce the general version using Zariski descent and
Lemma

A.0.3
.

∎

To generalise this result to arbitrary coefficients, at least in the case where
X
is proper, we first observe that de Rham cohomology with
F
-gauge coefficients is also equipped with a crystalline Frobenius, just as in the case with trivial coefficients:

Remark 3.5.3
.

Let
X
be a bounded
p
-adic formal scheme and
E\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
an
F
-gauge on
X
. Then the rational de Rham cohomology
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]
is equipped with an automorphism
\tau
linear over the Frobenius of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in the following way: By the stacky version of the crystalline comparison, i.e. by
\varphi^{*}\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}
, see
[
6
, Rem. 2.5.12, Constr. 3.1.1]
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\varphi^{*}R\Gamma{\Delta}(X_{p=0},j{\Delta}^{*}T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]=\varphi^{*}j{\Delta}^{*}\pi_{(X_{p=0})^{\mathrm{Syn}},*}T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;.}

However, by (
3.3.3
), the complex
j{\Delta}^{*}\pi_{(X_{p=0})^{\mathrm{Syn}},*}T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]
is naturally equipped with a Frobenius automorphism since it is pulled back from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
along
j{\Delta}
. We call the corresponding automorphism
\tau
of
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]
the
crystalline Frobenius
.

Theorem 3.5.4
.

Let
E\in\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
. Then the Hodge-filtered de Rham map induces a fibre sequence

R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{1-\tau}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;,

where
\tau
denotes
the crystalline Frobenius from
Remark

3.5.3
.

Proof.

Recall from
Lemma

3.3.1
that there is a fibre sequence

R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta},j{\Delta}^{*}T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{1-\tau}R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta},j{\Delta}^{*}T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;.

Again using the stacky version
\varphi^{*}\mathbb{F}_{p}{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}}
of the crystalline comparison as in
Remark

3.5.3
, the fibre sequence above becomes

R\Gamma(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{1-\tau}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]

and hence
Theorem

3.0.2
implies that it suffices to show that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fib(R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{1-\tau}R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]^{\tau=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}

is a pullback diagram. For this, let
M
denote the term in the upper left corner and write

N\coloneqq R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]/R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;.

Since we already know that
all rows and columns except the leftmost column in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]^{\tau=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\frac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\tau}

are fibre sequences, we conclude that also the left column is a fibre sequence and this finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.5.5
.

Let
X
be a
p
-adic formal scheme which is smooth and proper over
\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. For any perfect
F
-gauge
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is a natural fibre sequence

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,E)[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow\Fil^{0}_{\mathrm{Hod}}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\xrightarrow{1-\tau}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X,T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\;,

where
\tau
again denotes the crystalline Frobenius from
Remark

3.5.3
.

Proof.

Using
Proposition

B.0.1
, this follows immediately from
Theorem

3.5.4
.

∎

In particular, by the comparison from
Theorem

2.2.10
, the above recovers the result of
Theorem

3.5.2
in the proper case by putting
E=\mathcal{O}\{i\}
.

Remark 3.5.6
.

In fact, the result of
Theorem

3.5.2
holds already integrally for
i\leq p-2
. This integral version can also be obtained via
F
-gauges as shown in
[
43
, Rem. 5.21]
and
[
6
, Rem. 6.5.15]
, hence
Theorem

3.5.4
admits an integral analogue for perfect
F
-gauges
E
with Hodge–Tate weights all at least
-(p-2)
.

3.6.
The isogeny category of perfect
F
-gauges on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

Taking the commutative square from
Proposition

3.1.1
and passing to categories of perfect complexes, we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{crys}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{dR},+}}

of stable
\infty
-categories and hence a functor

\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})\rightarrow\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR},+})\times_{\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{dR}})}\Perf(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})\;.

Passing to isogeny categories and composing with the functors
from (
3.3.2
) and (
3.3.7
),
we obtain a functor

\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow\mathcal{DF}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times_{\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\mathcal{D}(\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))\;.

The category on the right-hand side (or, more precisely, a small variant thereof) has been called the
category of
p
-adic Hodge complexes
in
[
4
]
. In this section, we will show that the Beilinson fibre square admits the following categorical extension:

Theorem 3.6.1
.

The functor

\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\rightarrow\mathcal{DF}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times_{\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\mathcal{D}(\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))

described above is a fully faithful embedding.

On our way of proving the above result,
we will also obtain the following statement, which we learnt from Bhargav Bhatt in private communication, see also
[
6
, Rem. 6.7.5]
:

Proposition 3.6.2
.

The functor
T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}
induces an equivalence of categories

\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\mathcal{D}^{b}(\Rep^{\mathrm{crys}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}))

between the isogeny category of perfect complexes on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
and the bounded derived category of crystalline
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representations of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

Proof.

To prove full faithfulness, it suffices to show that, for any
E_{1},E_{2}\in\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, we have

\RHom(E_{1},E_{2})[\tfrac{1}{p}]\cong\RHom_{\MF_{\mathrm{adm}}^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(D_{1},D_{2})\;,

where
D_{i}\coloneqq D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E_{i})[\tfrac{1}{p}])
for
i=1,2
.
For
E_{1}=\mathcal{O}
, we already know this: indeed, as all functors in sight commute with shifts and fibres, we may assume that
E_{2}
is a coherent sheaf by an application of
Lemma

3.4.3
and then the claim follows from the proof of
Theorem

3.0.2
. However, the special case
E_{1}=\mathcal{O}
now implies the general case: since
also
\underline{\RHom}(E_{1},E_{2})
is a perfect complex, the tensor-hom adjunction yields

\begin{split}\RHom(E_{1},E_{2})[\tfrac{1}{p}]&\cong\RHom(\mathcal{O},\underline{\mathrm{RHom}}(E_{1},E_{2}))[\tfrac{1}{p}]\\
&\cong\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\underline{\mathrm{RHom}}(E_{1},E_{2}))[\tfrac{1}{p}]))\\
&\cong\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\underline{\mathrm{RHom}}_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(D_{1},D_{2}))\\
&\cong\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(D_{1},D_{2})\;,\end{split}

as desired. Note that we have used that

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\underline{\mathrm{RHom}}(E_{1},E_{2}))[\tfrac{1}{p}])\cong\underline{\mathrm{RHom}}_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(D_{1},D_{2})\;;

this
follows from the fact that
D_{\mathrm{crys}}
is
a symmetric monoidal equivalence
while the étale realisation is the composition of a pullback along an open immersion with a symmetric monoidal equivalence,
both of which preserve internal hom

between perfect complexes
. Finally,
by
Lemma

3.4.3
,
essential surjectivity is immediate from
Theorem

2.4.14
and the fact that the étale realisation preserves fibres
(since it commutes with shifts and colimits)
.
∎

From here, it is quite straightforward to prove
Theorem

3.6.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.6.1
.

Since
\mathcal{DF}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\mathcal{D}(\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
, all we have is to show is that, for any
E_{1},E_{2}\in\Perf(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, the given functor induces a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom(E_{1},E_{2})[\tfrac{1}{p}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E_{1})[\tfrac{1}{p}],T_{\mathrm{crys}}(E_{2})[\tfrac{1}{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E_{1})[\tfrac{1}{p}],T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E_{2})[\tfrac{1}{p}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E_{1})[\tfrac{1}{p}],T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E_{2})[\tfrac{1}{p}])\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

However,
using
Proposition

3.6.2
and
writing
D_{i}=D_{\mathrm{crys}}(T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E_{i})[\tfrac{1}{p}])
for
i=1,2
again, it follows from
Lemma

3.3.2
and
Lemma

3.3.4
that it is equivalent to prove that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF^{\varphi}_{\mathrm{adm}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(D_{1},D_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\Mod^{\varphi}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(T_{\mathrm{crys}}(D_{1}),T_{\mathrm{crys}}(D_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\MF(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(D_{1}),T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(D_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RHom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(T_{\mathrm{dR}}(D_{1}),T_{\mathrm{dR}}(D_{2}))}

is cartesian. However, this follows from
Proposition

3.2.3
using the tensor-hom adjunction and the fact that
T_{\mathrm{crys}},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}
and
T_{\mathrm{dR}}
commute with internal hom.
∎

4.
Comparing syntomic cohomology and étale cohomology

In this section, we prove a comparison theorem between syntomic cohomology and étale cohomology with coefficients in sufficiently negative Hodge–Tate weights. This generalises previous results by Abhinandan, Colmez–Nizioł and Tsuji, see
[
1
, Cor. 1.11]
,
[
15
, Cor. 5.21]
and
[
42
, Thm. 3.3.4]
. Most notably, while the aforementioned results all concern the rational case, i.e. one needs to invert
p
, this is not necessary in our setting and our results hold integrally.

Theorem 4.0.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth qcqs
p
-adic formal scheme of relative dimension
d
over
\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. For any
F
-gauge
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
with Hodge–Tate weights all at most
-d-2
, there is a natural isomorphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,E)\cong R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{\eta},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))\;.

If we restrict to vector bundle
F
-gauges, we can obtain an even finer comparison. However, note that the range of our result differs slightly from those of the results of Colmez–Nizioł and Tsuji mentioned above, which is due to the fact that they are working with log structures; see also the discussion in
[
1
, Rem.s 1.3, 1.12.(i)]
.

Theorem 4.0.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth qcqs
p
-adic formal scheme. For any vector bundle
F
-gauge
E\in\Vect(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
with Hodge–Tate weights all at most
-i-1
for some
i\geq 0
, the natural morphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,E)\rightarrow R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{\eta},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))

induces an isomorphism

\tau^{\leq i}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,E)\cong\tau^{\leq i}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{\eta},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))

and an injection on
H^{i+1}
.

4.1.
The reduced locus of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}

To prove
Theorem

4.0.1
and
Theorem

4.0.2
, we will define and describe a certain locus inside
X^{\mathrm{N}}
and
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, similarly to what we have done for the reduced locus of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. For the rest of this section, let
X
be a bounded
p
-adic formal scheme.

Definition 4.1.1
.

The
reduced locus

X_{\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
is defined as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{Syn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Similarly, we define the reduced locus
X_{\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{N}}
of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
.

Alternatively, one may describe the reduced locus of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
as the locus cut out by the equations
p=v_{1,X}=0
, where
v_{1,X}\in H^{0}(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},\mathcal{O}\{p-1\}/p)
denotes the pullback of the section
v_{1}\in H^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},\mathcal{O}\{p-1\}/p)
to
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. Then
X_{\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{N}}
can also be obtained as the pullback of
X_{\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to
X^{\mathrm{N}}
.

To pass between cohomology on
X_{\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
and
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, we again introduce a syntomic filtration similarly to the case
X=\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}

treated in
Definition

2.4.19
:

Definition 4.1.2
.

Let
E\in\mathcal{D}((X^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
. The filtration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E\{-(p-1)\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E\{p-1\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1,X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1,X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1,X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1,X}}

is called the
syntomic filtration
and denoted
\Fil_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{Syn}}R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\frac{1}{v_{1}}]
. We denote the underlying unfiltered object by
R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\frac{1}{v_{1}}]
.

Note that, similarly to (
2.4.7
), for any
E\in\mathcal{D}((X^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
, there is a canonical isomorphism

(4.1.1)

\gr^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\bullet}R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\cong R\Gamma(X_{\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}},E/v_{1,X}\{\bullet(p-1)\})\;,

where
E/v_{1,X}\coloneqq\cofib(E\{-(p-1)\}\xrightarrow{v_{1,X}}E)
. Moreover, as in the case
X=\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, see (
2.4.8
), we have the following result relating the syntomic filtration to étale cohomology:

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
X
be a bounded
qcqs

p
-adic formal scheme. For any
E\in\mathcal{D}((X^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
,
the syntomic filtration is complete
.
If
X
is moreover
p
-quasisyntomic and
E\in\Perf((X^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0})
, then
there is a natural isomorphism

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\cong R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{\eta},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))\;.

Proof.

We work over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
throughout and thus mostly suppress the subscript
(-)_{p=0}
from the notation. We first claim that the section
v_{1,X}
is topologically nilpotent, i.e. its pullback to any scheme
\Spec S\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
is nilpotent; as
v_{1,X}
arises from
v_{1}
via pullback, it suffices to prove this for
X=\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. However, recalling that we have defined
v_{1}
as the pullback of the section
u^{p}t
of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(-1)\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(p)
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{+}/\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\mathrm{dR}}
, it suffices to show that
(ut)^{p}
is topologically nilpotent on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
. As this is the pullback of the section
t^{p}:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(-p)
along the composite map

\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\xrightarrow{(t,u)}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{+}/\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\mathrm{dR}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{mult}}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\mathrm{dR}}\;,

it suffices to show that the pullback of
t^{p}
along
\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\mu}}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\mathrm{dR}}
is topologically nilpotent and we may do this after a further pullback along the fpqc cover
F:\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}
. Using the commutative diagram (it is in fact a pullback square)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}

from
Example

2.3.10
, it is then enough to check that the map
\mu:\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
factors through
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
, but this is true by
Example

2.3.4
.

To prove the second statement, first observe that there is a natural map

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\rightarrow R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{\eta},T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E))

as the étale realisation carries
v_{1,X}
to an isomorphism by
[
6
, Ex. 6.3.3]
. To show that this map is an isomorphism,
Lemma

A.0.3
allows us to reduce to the case where
X=\Spf R
is quasiregular semiperfectoid using quasisyntomic descent and
Lemma

2.3.20
. However, in this case, the claim follows from the description of the étale realisation functor from
[
6
, Constr. 6.3.1]
.
∎

4.2.
The components of the reduced locus of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}

We now explain how to obtain the reduced locus
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}}
by gluing several components which have a fairly explicit description.
Before proceeding, we encourage the reader to revisit the description of the reduced locus
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
given towards the end of Section
2.4
as the case
X=\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
will be prototypical.
We let
Y\coloneqq X_{p=0}
denote the special fibre of
X
.

Definition 4.2.1
.

The
de Rham component

X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR}}
of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
is defined as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Note that, by definition of
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}
, the map
i_{\mathrm{dR}}
induces an fpqc cover
\Spec\mathbb{F}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}
which classifies the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-point
(\mathbb{F}_{p}\xrightarrow{\id}\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\xrightarrow{0}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{F}_{p}),W(\mathbb{F}_{p})\xrightarrow{p}W(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
and it is straightforward to check that the pullback of
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{N}}
along this cover identifies with
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{dR}}
. Thus, we see that this induces a pullback square

(4.2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\mathrm{dR}})_{p=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathbb{F}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Remark 4.2.2
.

If
X
is smooth and qcqs, using (
4.2.1
)
and
Remark

2.2.6
, one can give an explicit description of vector bundles on
X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR}}
in terms of vector bundles on
Y
with a flat connection
having locally nilpotent
p
-curvature
equipped with an additional Sen operator
\Theta
satisfying certain compatibilities.

Although we will not need it in the sequel, we also shortly want to discuss the Hodge–Tate component
X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT}}
defined as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT}}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

To this end, recall that
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT}}^{\mathrm{N}}
can be described as
j_{\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta})\cap\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}}=j_{\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\Delta})\cap(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}})_{p=t=0}
and hence
X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT}}
identifies with
(X{\Delta})_{p=\mu=0}
via the map
j_{\mathrm{HT}}
. In other words, we have
X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT}}\cong(X^{\mathrm{HT}})_{p=0}
, where
X^{\mathrm{HT}}
is the
Hodge–Tate stack
of
X
, see
[
8
, Constr. 3.7]
, and this means that there is a pullback square

(4.2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\not}{D})_{p=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathbb{F}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT}}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;,}}

where
X^{\not}{D}
denotes the
diffracted Hodge stack
of
X
as introduced in
[
8
, Constr. 3.8]
, see also
[
27
, Def. 3.2.1]
, and the lower map classifies the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-point
(\mathbb{F}_{p}\xrightarrow{0}\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\xrightarrow{\id}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{F}_{p}),W(\mathbb{F}_{p})\xrightarrow{p}W(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
.

Definition 4.2.3
.

The
Hodge-filtered de Rham component

X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR},+}
of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
is defined as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR},+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Note that, by definition of
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}
, there is an fpqc cover
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}
induced by the Hodge-filtered de Rham map and it is straightforward to check that the pullback of
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{N}}
along this cover identifies with
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{dR},+}
. In other words, this cover induces a pullback square

(4.2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\mathrm{dR},+})_{p=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{dR},+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Remark 4.2.4
.

If
X
is smooth and qcqs, using (
4.2.3
)
and
Remark

2.2.12
, one can describe vector bundles on
X_{\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}
explicitly in terms of filtered vector bundles on
Y
equipped with a flat connection satisfying Griffiths transversality and a Sen operator.

Definition 4.2.5
.

The
conjugate-filtered Hodge–Tate component

X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT},c}
of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
is defined as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT},c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Recalling that
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a,+}^{\mathrm{dR}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we see that there is an fpqc cover
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{+}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}}
and, by definition of the
conjugate-filtered diffracted Hodge stack

X^{\not{D},c}
of
X
as introduced in
[
27
, Def. 3.3.1]
, this cover induces a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\not{D},c})_{p=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{HT},c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}_{+}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Definition 4.2.6
.

The
Hodge component

X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{Hod}}
of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
is defined as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{Hod}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Note that base changing the fpqc cover
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}
from above to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}
yields an fpqc cover
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}
. Moreover, from the pullback square (
4.2.3
), we infer that there is a pullback square

(4.2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{Hod}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

For our analysis of the syntomic cohomology of
X
, it will be vital to have a good understanding of vector bundles on
X_{\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}
and their cohomology. For this, we will first need to recall some facts about Higgs bundles. Thus, let
Y
be a scheme or formal scheme which is smooth over a base
(formal) scheme

S
. For us, the case
S=\Spec\mathbb{F}_{p}
will be the most relevant as we will want to apply the following definitions in our context of
Y
being the the special fibre of some smooth
p
-adic formal scheme
X
.

Definition 4.2.7
.

Let
V\in\Vect(Y)
be a vector bundle on
Y
. A
Higgs field
on
V
is an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-linear morphism
\Phi:V\rightarrow V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{1}
such that
\Phi\wedge\Phi=0
. Here,
\Phi\wedge\Phi:V\rightarrow V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{2}
denotes the composition

V\xrightarrow{\mathrlap{\hskip 7.11317pt\Phi}\hphantom{\Phi\otimes\id}}V\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\xrightarrow{\Phi\otimes\id}V\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\xrightarrow{\id\otimes\wedge}V\otimes\Omega^{2}_{Y/S}\;,

where
\wedge:\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\rightarrow\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\wedge\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\cong\Omega^{2}_{Y/S}
is the canonical map.

From a Higgs field
\Phi
on some
V\in\Vect(Y)
, we obtain induced morphisms
\Phi:V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{i}\rightarrow V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{i+1}
given by

V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{i}\xrightarrow{\Phi\otimes\id}V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{1}\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{i}\xrightarrow{\id\otimes\wedge}V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{i+1}

and thus a variant of the Hodge complex

V\xrightarrow{\Phi}V\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\xrightarrow{\Phi}V\otimes\Omega^{2}_{Y/S}\xrightarrow{\Phi}\dots\;.

By the tensor-hom adjunction, for any vector bundle
V
on
Y
, an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-linear morphism
\Phi:V\rightarrow V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{1}
is equivalent to the data of an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-linear morphism

\Phi_{(-)}:\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(V)\;,

which can explicitly be described as sending any local section
\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}(U)
to the endomorphism

\Phi_{\theta}:V|_{U}\xrightarrow{\mathrlap{\hskip 8.5359pt\Phi}\hphantom{\id\otimes\theta^{\vee}}}(V\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S})|_{U}\xrightarrow{\id\otimes\theta^{\vee}}V|_{U}\;;

here,
\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}
denotes the tangent bundle of
Y
over
S
, as usual.
One can then check that the condition
\Phi\wedge\Phi=0
is exactly equivalent to requiring that
\Phi_{\theta}
and
\Phi_{\theta^{\prime}}
commute for any local sections
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}(U)
. Thus, we conclude that equipping
V
with a Higgs field is equivalent to equipping
V
with the structure of a
\Sym^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}
-module,
where we remind the reader that
\Sym^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}
denotes the symmetric algebra of the
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module
\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}
. Finally, we say that a Higgs field
\Theta
on
V\in\Vect(Y)
is
locally nilpotent
if for any local sections
\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}(U),s\in V(U)
, we have
\Phi_{\theta}^{n}(s)=0
for some
n>0
.

Finally, we note that the above definitions immediately generalise to the case of arbitrary quasi-coherent complexes
V\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
instead of vector bundles: A Higgs bundle on
Y
is an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-linear morphism
\Phi:V\otimes\Omega_{Y/S}^{1}\rightarrow\Omega_{Y/S}^{1}
and this is equivalent to the datum of equipping
V
with the structure of a
\Sym^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}
-complex; it is locally nilpotent if for any local section
\theta\in\mathcal{T}_{Y/S}(U)
, the operator
\Phi_{\theta}
acts locally nilpotently on the sections of the cohomology sheaves of
V
over
U
. Again, we obtain an analogue of the Hodge complex given by the total complex

\Tot(V\xrightarrow{\Phi}V\otimes\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\xrightarrow{\Phi}V\otimes\Omega^{2}_{Y/S}\xrightarrow{\Phi}\dots)\;.

We now return to our particular case of
Y=X_{p=0}
for a smooth
p
-adic formal scheme
X
. Using the pullback square (
4.2.4
), it turns out that the main part of the work lies in analysing vector bundles on
(X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0}
and their cohomology.

Lemma 4.2.8
.

Let
X
be a smooth qcqs
p
-adic formal scheme
and put
Y=X_{p=0}
. Then there is an isomorphism

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0}\cong B_{Y\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}}\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{T}_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(1))^{\sharp}}

over
Y\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Proof.

Note that, over the stack
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, the map
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{Hod}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}\oplus B\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{O}(1))^{\sharp}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}
is a square-zero extension of the target by
B\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{O}(1))^{\sharp}
and thus the induced map

(X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0}\rightarrow(B\mathbb{G}_{m})_{p=0}

of stacks over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a
\mathbb{V}(\mathcal{T}_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(1))^{\sharp}
-gerbe
,
where
\mathcal{T}_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}
denotes the tangent sheaf of
Y
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, by derived deformation theory.
4
4

4

More precisely, we are using the following assertion in derived algebraic geometry: Let
Y
be a finite type
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme and
A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
a square-zero extension of animated
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebras with fibre
N\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}(A)
. Then the fibre of the map
Y(A^{\prime})\rightarrow Y(A)
over a point
\eta:\Spec A\rightarrow X
is a torsor for
\operatorname{Der}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\mathcal{O}_{Y},\eta_{*}N)\cong\Map_{A}(\eta^{*}L_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}},N)
;
the proof is similar to
[
16
, Thm. 5.1.13]
.
Note that, if furthermore
N=L[1]\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq-1}(A)
and
Y
is smooth, we have
\Map_{A}(\eta^{*}L_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}},N)\cong B(\eta^{*}\mathcal{T}_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}\otimes_{A}L)
.
However, as the square-zero extension above splits via the canonical injection, the gerbe is also split and we are done.
∎

Due to the description of
(X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0}
provided by the lemma above, we will make use of the following general statement about
\mathbb{V}(E)^{\sharp}
-representations for a vector bundle
E
:

Lemma 4.2.9
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
E\in\Vect(X)
a vector bundle on
X
. Then there is a natural equivalence

\mathcal{D}(B\mathbb{V}(E)^{\sharp})\cong\mathcal{D}(\widehat{\mathbb{V}(E^{\vee})})

of stable
\infty
-categories
compatible with the forgetful functors on both sides; here,
\widehat{\mathbb{V}(E^{\vee})}
denotes the completion of
\mathbb{V}(E^{\vee})
at the zero section. Moreover, if
V
is a quasi-coherent complex on
B\mathbb{V}(E)^{\sharp}
and
\pi:B\mathbb{V}(E)^{\sharp}\rightarrow X
is the projection, then

\pi_{*}V\cong\Tot(V\rightarrow V\otimes E^{\vee}\rightarrow V\otimes\wedge^{2}E^{\vee}\rightarrow\dots)\;,

where the maps are induced by the natural
\Sym^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}E
-module structure on
V
via the equivalence above. Note that we abuse notation and also use
V
to denote the pullback of
V
to
X
.

Proof.

The first assertion is
[
6
, Prop. 2.4.5]
. For the second one, we reduce to the case where
X=\Spec R
is affine. Then we have

\pi_{*}V=R\Gamma(B\mathbb{V}(E)^{\sharp},V)\cong\RHom_{B\mathbb{V}(E)^{\sharp}}(\mathcal{O},V)\cong\RHom_{\Sym^{\bullet}_{R}E}(R,V)\;,

where the action of
E
on
R
is trivial in the rightmost term. Using the Koszul resolution

(\dots\rightarrow\wedge^{2}E\otimes_{R}\Sym^{\bullet}_{R}E\rightarrow E\otimes_{R}\Sym^{\bullet}_{R}E\rightarrow\Sym^{\bullet}_{R}E)\rightarrow R

to compute the RHom above, we obtain the result.
∎

Proposition 4.2.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth qcqs
p
-adic formal scheme
and put
Y=X_{p=0}
. Then giving a quasi-coherent complex on
(X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0}
amounts to specifying a graded quasi-coherent complex
V=\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}
on
Y
equipped with a Higgs field
\Phi:V\rightarrow V\otimes\Omega_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}^{1}
which is locally nilpotent and decreases degree by
1
, i.e. the restriction of
\Phi
to
V_{i}
comes with a factorisation through
V_{i-1}\otimes\Omega_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}^{1}
.

Proof.

This now follows by combining
Lemma

4.2.8

with the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant version of

Lemma

4.2.9
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.11
.

Let
X
be a smooth qcqs
p
-adic formal scheme
and put
Y=X_{p=0}
. If
E\in\mathcal{D}((X^{\mathrm{Hod}})_{p=0})
corresponds to the data
(V_{\bullet},\Phi)
via
Proposition

4.2.10
, then the pushforward of
E
to
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
identifies with the graded complex whose degree
i
term is given by

R\Gamma(Y,\Tot(V_{i}\xrightarrow{\Phi}V_{i-1}\otimes\Omega_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}^{1}\xrightarrow{\Phi}V_{i-2}\otimes\Omega_{Y/\mathbb{F}_{p}}^{2}\xrightarrow{\Phi}\dots))\;.

Proof.

Immediate from
Lemma

4.2.8
and
the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant version of

Lemma

4.2.9
.
∎

Finally, let us explain how to obtain
X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{red}}
and
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}}
from the components above by gluing: From the analogous statements for
X=\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we see that gluing
X_{\mathrm{dR},+}^{\mathrm{N}}
and
X_{\mathrm{HT},c}^{\mathrm{N}}
along
X_{\mathrm{Hod}}^{\mathrm{N}}
yields the stack
X^{\mathrm{N}}_{\mathrm{red}}
. To obtain
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}}
, we then have to further glue
X_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{N}}
and
X_{\mathrm{HT}}^{\mathrm{N}}
along a Frobenius twist.

4.3.
Proof of the main theorems

We now move on to proving our main statements concerning the comparison between syntomic cohomology and étale cohomology announced in the beginning. As in the case
X=\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we use the notation
F_{\mathrm{dR}}(-),F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(-),F_{\mathrm{dR},+}(-)
and
F_{\mathrm{HT},c}(-)
to denote cohomology on the components of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.0.2
.

Let
d
be the relative dimension of
X
over
\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We are going to show that
\cofib(R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,E)\rightarrow R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,T_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(E)))
is concentrated in degrees at least
i+1
,
which suffices to establish the statement
. By
p
-completeness, it suffices to check this after reducing mod
p
, i.e. we may assume that
E
is a vector bundle on
(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0}
. Subsequently,
Proposition

4.1.3
reduces us to proving that the complexes
\gr^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{k}R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]
are concentrated in
cohomological
degrees at least
i+1
for all
k\geq 1

as this will imply that the cofibre of the map

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]

is concentrated in cohomological degrees at least
i+1
, from which our result follows.

Using the isomorphism

\gr^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{k}R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\cong R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}},E/v_{1,X}\{k(p-1)\})

from (
4.1.1
), we now replace
E
by
E/v_{1,X}\{k(p-1)\}
; then our assumption becomes that
E
is a vector bundle on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}}
with Hodge–Tate weights all at most
-i-p
and we have to
prove that
R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}},E)
is concentrated in
cohomological
degrees at least
i+1
. For this, we identify the pushforward
\pi_{X^{\mathrm{Syn}},*}E
of
E
to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,\mathrm{red}}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
with the data
(V,\Fil^{\bullet}V,\Fil_{\bullet}V)
with
\gr^{\bullet}V=\gr_{\bullet}V
equipped with operators
D
and
\Theta
according to the description of the reduced locus of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. Now we make the following observation: by
Proposition

4.2.11
, the Hodge–Tate weights of
\pi_{X^{\mathrm{Syn}},*}E
are all at most
-i-p+d
; moreover, for
0\leq k\leq d
, the complex
\gr^{-i-p+d-k}V
is concentrated in degrees at least
d-k
. In particular, we deduce that

F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(E)=\fib(\gr^{0}V\xrightarrow{\Theta}\gr^{-p}V)

is concentrated in degrees at least
i+1
. As the filtration
\Fil^{\bullet}V
is
complete by
Lemma

B.0.3
and base change for the cartesian square (
4.2.3
)
, we may also conclude that
\Fil^{0}V
is concentrated in degrees at least
i+p
while
\Fil^{-p}V
is concentrated in degrees at least
i
. Thus, the complex

F_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)=\fib(\Fil^{0}V\xrightarrow{\Theta}\Fil^{-p}V)

is concentrated in degrees at least
i+1
. Finally, we may also infer that the natural map
\Fil_{-1}V\rightarrow V
is an isomorphism in cohomological degrees at most
i+p-1
while

\Fil_{0}V\rightarrow V

is an isomorphism in cohomological degrees at most
i+p
. Thus, the natural map

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}F_{\mathrm{HT},c}(E)=\fib(\Fil_{0}V\xrightarrow{D}\Fil_{-1}V)\rightarrow\fib(V\xrightarrow{\Theta}V)=F_{\mathrm{dR}}(E)}

is also an isomorphism in cohomological degrees at most
i+p
. Overall, using the formula

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}},E)=\fib(F_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E)\oplus F_{\mathrm{HT},c}(E)\xrightarrow{a_{E}-b_{E}}F_{\mathrm{dR}}(E)\oplus F_{\mathrm{Hod}}(E))

from (
2.4.6
), we see that
R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}},E)
is indeed concentrated in degrees at least
i+1
, as wanted.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.0.1
.

As in the
proof of
Theorem

4.0.2
, we may assume that
E
is a vector bundle on
(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})_{p=0}
by
p
-completeness of both sides and then
Proposition

4.1.3
reduces us to proving that

\gr^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{k}R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)[\tfrac{1}{v_{1}}]\cong R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}},E/v_{1,X}\{k(p-1)\})

vanishes for all
k\geq 1
, where we have again used (
4.1.1
). Replacing
E
by
E/v_{1,X}\{k(p-1)\}
, we thus have to show that,
for
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}})
with Hodge–Tate weights all at most
-p-d-1
, the cohomology
R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{\mathrm{red}},E)
vanishes. As before, we identify
\pi_{X^{\mathrm{Syn}},*}E
with the data
(V,\Fil^{\bullet}V,\Fil_{\bullet}V)
with
\gr^{\bullet}V=\gr_{\bullet}V
equipped with operators
D
and
\Theta
. From
Proposition

4.2.11
, we see that
